These Bangkok Hotels Offer In-House Experiences, Beautiful Views, and Excellent Bang for Your Buck

📅 2018-12-13  |  Read Source →

After 14 years in Bangkok, I’ve watched the city evolve into one of the most electric destinations in Southeast Asia. It’s a city of duality—constantly on the move with new hotel openings, world-class restaurants, and an ever-changing shopping scene, but with tradition firmly at its core.

There's something in Bangkok for every kind of traveler, no matter your age or budget. You can enjoy a lunch made by an 80-year-old auntie still serving the same streetside crab omelet she has for decades, or dine at the only three-Michelin-star restaurant in Thailand for dinner. Ancient golden temples stand alongside modern shopping centers, and old shophouses are being revived by the younger generation who want to preserve the city's heritage. When it comes to the hotel scene, this dichotomy is also in play. Guests can choose between historic boutique properties or glamorous new builds, and find every kind of amenity, design style, or cuisine under the sun.

Just as captivating is the warmth and hospitality of the Thai people, something I’ve found to be unlike anywhere else in the world. Thailand is known as the “Land of Smiles,” and from my experience, it more than lives up to its name. The city reveals itself through small, thoughtful moments mixed with grand experiences, all set to an energetic pace that makes it one of my favorite destinations. If you're ready to see it all for yourself, read on for some of the best hotels in Thailand. —Megan Leon

The 30-story tower that this hotel calls home cuts a striking figure in the Bangkok skyline, shaped as it is after the wai, a Thai gesture of greeting made by pressing one’s palms together—a fitting shape for a place that beautifully melds Thai hospitality with a hyper-polished service style. The staff here is supremely gracious: They remember names, press buttons for guests at the elevator banks, turn tables over quickly at breakfast, and happily fetch booster seats for little princes and princesses. And if that’s not enough to make you feel at home, perhaps the stylish rooms and common spaces will, designed as they are to offer a true sense of place. Modern sculptures and paintings by living Thai artists, and subtle design nods to the country’s silk-making history and garments worn by traditional Thai dancers, delight at every turn. —Matt Ortile

It feels like you might be in London when you're at The Peninsula in Bangkok's Thonburi neighborhood, thanks in part to the British racing vehicles parked out front. But the hotel's custom tuk-tuk reminds you that you're in Thailand, and rich, warm toffee tones carry throughout the lobby and lounges. The space offers grand views over the Chao Phraya and the Thai river homes across the water. The Peninsula swimming pool is among the best in the city; there's no better way to beat the heat in this famously hot capital than by taking a dip right on the river. —J.A.

In a city where almost every high-end hotel brand has already made a mark, it takes something extra special to turn heads. Yet when Capella Bangkok finally opened after a seven-year tease, the city’s high-society set swooped in like magpies. Why? Capella’s first foray in Thailand is the only city hotel with villas right on the Chao Phraya River—multi-roomed retreats with private gardens and swimming pools lined with travertine marble. Suites in the main building—a blocky, low-slung affair with 101 rooms—come with plunge pools on their balconies, and the hotel tapped hot-shot chef Mauro Colagreco (of Mirazur in France) to head its signature coastal Mediterranean restaurant, Côte. Crystalline coupes of private-label Champagne accompany check-in, brunches, and turn-down service, but that’s as flashy as it gets. The rest feels decidedly subdued. Service is hushed but switched-on, and the spa eschews New Age treatments for humble, age-old healing practices (tok sen hammer massages, luk pra kob herbal compresses) inspired by the historic Thai-Chinese community just outside its gates. —C.S.

Compared with many of its rivals, this Bangkok stalwart doesn’t have a location that’s wildly convenient: The postcard sights need a boat transfer, and most top tables are more than a 30-minute taxi ride away. But for the legions of loyal fans, that’s the point. Pitched up on a lush riverside plot in the hushed Dusit district, The Siam feels like a portal to a bygone Bangkok. Even though it opened over a decade ago, it’s easy to imagine it as a plush playground for the city’s erstwhile big guns. You'd be hard-pressed to point friends to a lovelier lunch spot than the hotel’s Thai restaurant, set between the timeworn pillars of three ancient teakwood houses, where butlers in dressy black sarongs deliver the kind of classic Thai hospitality—lilting “sawadee ka” greetings and tables set with fresh orchids—that’s becoming harder to find. None of which is to say that the hotel is stuffy—there’s a tattoo parlor in the spa. —Chris Schalkx

Making its debut in 2025 as the second Andaz outpost in Thailand, Andaz One Bangkok is an urban escape that deftly balances its proximity to Bangkok’s largest green park with its connection to the city’s fast-paced energy. Set in the Lumphini neighborhood, it offers direct views of leafy Lumphini Park while also keeping guests within reach of the business and shopping areas nearby. Rooted in Thai art, local female artists shine throughout the hotel, giving the space the feel of a gallery. Social spaces, ranging from the lobby lounge to the rooftop restaurant, make this the perfect place to relax and connect with other travelers. —M.L.

Opened in August 2026, Fairmont Bangkok Sukhumvit marks the Fairmont brand's debut in Thailand. The hotel is a comfortable base for exploring Sukhumvit, with public transportation and the Em District within walking distance, while its on-site restaurants, bars, and outdoor pool give plenty of reasons to stay in. Families are welcome, with a kids club and family-friendly pool complete with a waterslide. The interiors are modern and luxurious without losing their warmth, with thoughtful touches like the discreet, tucked-away reception desk and an impressive glass chandelier that is the talk of the town. For a hotel that's only been open a couple of months, the service is exceptional and thoughtful; by the end of the first day, everyone knows your name, making this new hotel feel like a luxe, warm, and welcoming home. —M.L.

The first hotel from Curio Collection by Hilton to arrive in Thailand, Kromo is a colorful celebration of Bangkok’s heritage with a contemporary edge. Located on Sukhumvit Road between Phrom Phong and Asoke, this 28-floor property is not what it seems from the outside; a modern black façade greets you at first glance, but once inside, a youthful pink-hued space opens up, with the hotel's lobby restaurant and bar just steps away. The hotel's design concept takes inspiration from a phrase in Bangkok's ceremonial Thai name, which translates to “The City of the Nine Gems,” brought to life in the creative social space Vilah (modeled after the chrysoberyl gem) and at the rooftop pool (designed after the zircon gem). Kromo sits at the foot of EM District, where three distinct shopping centers cater to every type of traveler. Across the way is one of Sukhumvit's most loved green parks for those needing a dose of nature and the occasional turtle sighting. —M.L.

The Dusit Thani Bangkok is in a class of its own, having first opened its doors five decades ago as one of the most glamorous and luxurious hotel experiences in Thailand. Following a full renovation, it reopened in 2024 with a refreshed Thai-inspired look while still holding on to the same warm, local hospitality that built its reputation. Located within the upper echelons of Silom, the hotel overlooks Lumphini Park, promising inescapable views from every room. Centrally located between the Chao Phraya River and the shopping mecca of Sukhumvit, guests have close access to everything from a rooftop garden to a retail center filled with local shops and popular dining spots. The property is also kid-friendly, with a kids club and cultural activities that are actually fun for little ones. —M.L.

Kimpton Maa-Lai manages to provide something not every large hotel can: a stay that’s both intimate yet connected to the city's lively social scene. The modern, stark façade is a contrast to what you’ll find inside—buzzing cafés, intimate bars, rare green space tucked into Bangkok's urban jungle, and spacious rooms with nature-inspired touches and Thai decor. On-site, there are six dining options, from a Michelin-starred restaurant to a moody bar. Plus, as a Kimpton, the hotel is pet-friendly; kids and adults of all ages will love spotting furry friends roaming the lobby with their owners. Located within a hip retail and dining village and just a few minutes from the rows of shopping centers along Sukhumvit Road, there’s much to do at your fingertips. —Megan Leon

The Ritz-Carlton's entry into Bangkok brought with it a serene stay in an evolving corner of the city with easy access to major sites and the brand's signature impeccable service. It sits on the One Bangkok campus, a $3 billion development with hotels, office buildings, condos, a massive shopping mall, an entertainment complex, and plenty of public art and outdoor space. Guest rooms begin on the eighth floor and go up to 24, half of which have balconies or outdoor space. Room types start at a Deluxe King or Deluxe Double, many with stand-alone tubs and walk-in rain showers, and expand up to The Ritz-Carlton Suite, a two-bedroom with a massive walk-in closet, dining room, bar, Lumphini Park views, and lounge access. Be sure to book a meal at Duet by David Toutain (whose eponymous Paris restaurant has two Michelin stars); his team serves modern French cuisine in the form of a seasonal tasting menu heavy on seafood, fresh vegetables, and ornate presentation. —Madison Flager

Romantic and right on the river, Amdaeng fits the bill if you want to relax and recharge away from the frenzy of the city. Inside, find interiors that include alligator-print leather sofas, a red swimming pool, bathtubs with chrome lion feet, Thai tilework, and Chinese antiques. Directly outside the hotel, wander the canals of the Thonburi District, crossing small bridges, visiting Buddhist spirit houses, and seeing the big banyan trees. Here, you'll get a taste of everyday life in Bangkok. You’ll find neighbors catching up curbside over noodles, and true hidden-gem restaurants. This hotel feels like a hidden discovery, tucked down an alley alongside a canal. —Jenny Adams

There’s no hotel quite like Bangkok Publishing Residence in the city, with its antique typewriters and cabin-chic rooms. Evoking the image of a Mad Men daydream, it's a time capsule of a former publishing heyday. With a treasure trove of antiquities and a lobby that preserves the building’s former life as a weekly series publisher, you won’t find anything like it anywhere. The owner inherited her grandfather’s printing house, restoring and reimagining it as a character-driven hotel with only eight rooms and miles of soul. If this is the first hotel you experience in Bangkok, it’s going to set a very high bar. But it can be tough to snag a stay, as this place sees frequent return guests. —J.A.

The Anantara Siam Bangkok Hotel, which draws a fashionable crowd of international travelers, is a verdant sanctuary in the retail jungle. Much to the delight of those staying here, the property has a stronger resort vibe than many hotels in this area, with tropical landscaping, a central swimming pool, gorgeous rooms, and old-world Thai details. It also offers some of the only terrace rooms in town; they come with adjoining gardens where you can sit out on a private patio. —J.A.

The Park Hyatt Bangkok has the feel of an art gallery, where striking design elements surprise you, but nothing gets in the way. You're pointed to the hotel entrance by a series of discreet signs in gold lettering, then elevators take you to the beautiful 10th-floor lobby, decorated in cream and white and accented with simple but bold artwork. Even the sleek, sculpted reception desk looks like modern art. The rooms are beautiful, and the bathrooms feel like spas. Penthouse Bar & Grill, which spans three upper floors of the building, was designed by the famous New York firm of AvroKo. The idea was to turn one seamless space into five separate venues with marble, ebony, and gold accents, plus antique British stylings. —Audrey Phoon

What are the best areas to stay in Bangkok?

The riverside neighborhoods of Rattanakosin and adjacent Banglamphu are a great starting point, with their famous temples like Wat Po, plus the Grand Palace complex, old forts, sunny parks, and the backpacker zone of Khao San Road. If you’re a fan of stiff drinks and sharp suits, visit Thonglor—often compared to Brooklyn—where a heavy Japanese expat community has resulted in great omakase joints, cool vintage purveyors, and a collection of high-end tailors. This is also the city’s nexus for hot new cocktail spots.

Chinatown’s tiny lane, Soi Nana, gets global nods for its handful of craft libation and beer bars, all cutting-edge but delightfully ramshackle. This neighborhood could take you a week to fully explore, jam-packed as it is with 300-year-old Taoist temples, darling coffee houses, serious dim sum, great shopping, and a maze of old-world food markets. If shopping and art are on the agenda, head to Siam, where you’ll find the city’s largest art museum, as well as a range of malls.

What’s the best way to get around Bangkok?

Bangkok’s traffic is infamous. However, if you plan accordingly, it’s hardly an issue. In the riverside neighborhoods and Chinatown, it’s easy to walk. The streets are flat and the sites are close together. Along Sukhumvit Road—the main artery that connects the mid-city neighborhoods like Thonglor, Siam, Prom Phong, and Sathorn—you can use the BTS SkyTrain. It’s simple to follow English maps, posted nearly everywhere, with colored lines and an easy ticket-purchasing process. This raised train runs every day from 6 a.m. to midnight and has three separate lines that connect huge portions of the city. Finally, make sure to hop in a tuk-tuk. Thailand’s famous rickshaw vehicles provide a fun, exhilarating way to experience the city.

I recommend visiting Bangkok for a minimum of three days, which will give you time to explore a few temples, get a taste of local street food in Chinatown, squeeze in a foot massage, and spend time gallery hopping at the new Dib Bangkok art museum or shopping at one of Bangkok’s many malls.

The best time to visit is from November through February, when the monsoon season has ended and the cooler season arrives—and by cooler, I just mean a degree or two less than usual.

Every hotel review on this list has been written by a Condé Nast Traveler journalist who knows the destination and has visited that property. When choosing hotels, our editors consider properties across price points that offer an authentic and insider experience of a destination, keeping design, location, service, and sustainability credentials top of mind.

We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

10 Best Subscription Boxes for Kids (2026): STEM, Books, Clothes, Snacks, and More

📅 2018-11-24  |  Read Source →

If you’re shopping for a unique gift idea this holiday season, these subscription boxes for kids are something young ones of all ages will get to enjoy not just on Christmas morning, but all year long. It can be something you know they’ll love, or just never-ending toys if it’s one of those ages where their interests are changing by the minute.

We’ve had all kinds of kid and parent testing with this guide. I’m the parent of a 4-year-old, while former WIRED senior editor Adrienne So tested kids subscription boxes with an 8-year-old and 10-year-old. Our latest recommendations were tested with a baby. Really, there’s no limit on what age these boxes can delight and teach. After years of testing, these are our favorites.

Looking for more kid gear? Don’t miss our guides to the Best STEM Toys, Best Baby Gear, and Best Kid Backpacks. Shopping for a parent? We’ve got gift guides for moms, dads, and new parents alike.

Updated November 2026: We’ve rearranged our top picks and updated our notes about Kiwi Crate and Lovevery’s subscription boxes. We also updated pricing information for all of our recommendations.

Kiwi Crate has a series of crates designed for different age groups, from the Panda Crate (designed for babies and toddlers under three) to the Maker and Eureka Crates aimed at 12 and up. Each box comes with a themed activity designed for its age group.

I tested out a Koala Crate, designed for ages three to six, with my 4-year-old son, and he's been loving every piece of it. It was themed around dinosaurs with a dinosaur bone dig kit and a dinosaur dress-up set, both of which my son loves. The dress-up set was four accessories (a headband, two hand pieces, and a tail on a belt) that made it easy for a variety of sized kids to use, and I loved that the dig kit came with two different dinosaur bone sets that fit into a little puzzle cut-out board when he's done digging.

Kiwi Crate's boxes come every month (except for the Panda Crate, which comes every two months), and the cost stays the same as you move through the different age groups—you won't be hit by a sudden price increase when your kid is older. —Nena Farrell

When I became a parent, I felt like I had a million things to do, or research how to do. My Lovevery subscription at least let me stop worrying about getting “the right toys.” While I only used it for the first 10 months, most of the toys were a huge hit with my kiddo. Not only did each kit include a variety of toys, each one included an entire booklet for parents about early childhood development, and how each toy was meant to stimulate your child.

During the first two months, my kiddo would stare at the high contrast cards of The Looker Play Kit constantly. Months three and four were dominate by the silicone teether toy and soft book inside The Charmer Play Kit. The stacking cups of The Inspector Play Kit quickly became (and remain) bath toys. While I cancelled after about 10 months, I would recommend this to any new parent with an empty toy shelf. —Harry Rabinowitz

Lalo’s Play Boxes are another good option made for the littlest people in our lives. There are 10 different boxes of toys that are designed for different months within the first two years of a kiddo’s life, starting at the zero-to-12-month infant stage. It’s available both as a subscription or a one-off purchase of a specific developmental window, such as three to four months or 16 to 18 months. You can even add it to a registry on Babylist.

The toys are beautifully made with nontoxic materials. I tried out two different Lalo Play Boxes and was happy to see that my son was usually interested in at least three of the items from each box (a good ratio for a toddler). Most of the toys we tested were made of wood, like the wooden blocks he likes to knock over from the 19 to 21 month box and the drum he plays from the 13 to 15 month set. I’m honestly jealous of how pretty some of the toys are and regularly resist the temptation to steal the cute Stacking Rocket for my bedside table.

Each box comes with a little Play Guide that describes how the toys are meant to be used and how they stimulate their specific age group. The booklet also gave tips on how to use the toys as your kid gets older, too. —Nena Farrell

My kids are 8 and 10, and they are obsessed with puzzles of all kinds. This is great because they are at the age when they need to practice reading, and there are only so many times you can force them to reenact The Lord of the Rings trilogy. The classic children's publisher Highlights also offers a few different reading and activity boxes, in addition to the iconic magazine. My kids and I tried the I Can Read book club ($13/month) and the Puzzlemania subscription ($4/month). Puzzlemania was delightful.

Each puzzle book is 32 pages and has a wide variety of puzzles, including sticker puzzles, word searches, Mad Libs–style storytelling, and riddles. We spent several afternoons filling in sticker puzzle pieces and playing the Highlights version of Sudoku. They both loved this puzzle pack so much that I bought the Highlights Great Big Book of Really Hard Puzzles ($8), which I'm anticipating will keep them occupied for at least one weekend trip or two. —Adrienne So

This is the best box. Kids need to snack every day anyway, so you might as well read and learn while you're at it. My kids tear into the Universal Yums snack box like a small pack of very tiny wolves. You can choose a small, medium, or large box, and it includes an informative booklet, games, and trivia.

Food is a great way to learn about geography and different cultures. Every month, my kids and I open the box, look at a globe, and do a lot of Googling. We have liked several foreign candies enough to start ordering them separately on Amazon. Even when the snacks aren't a hit—I got the salted truffle French fries all to myself—my kids always try them and ask a lot of questions about why a country is called the Kingdom of Butterflies and when we can visit. —Adrienne So

Cooking and baking subscription boxes are some of the best subscription boxes I've tried. Feeding kids every day is a chore, and I welcome any way to get some variety, even meal-delivery kits. Bake Eat Love is the first kit I was just as excited to try as my kids.

That said, the Bake Eat Love boxes are probably best suited for an older age group, with slightly more sophisticated palates. We tried the sampler box with tiramisu cupcakes. While I love the light, fluffy texture of tiramisu and the innovative recipe, my son was perturbed to find that a cupcake he assumed was chocolate tasted more like Mommy's coffee. The kit does include a few prepackaged ingredients, but as with most cooking and baking sets, you do have to have a basic set of cooking utensils and a few ingredients that might require an extra shopping trip, like mascarpone. —Adrienne So

Alternatives: I have gifted several Raddish Kids cooking club subscriptions to nieces and nephews. The recipes are extremely easy for little kids to follow (my children particularly loved making pretzels), and the included tiny utensils are adorable and perfect for tiny hands (and for moms who just like using tiny things). We also tried Eat2Explore, which was founded by a graduate of the French Culinary Institute. Each box features fun tools, nonperishable seasonings, recipe cards, and educational booklets from different countries. The salmon teriyaki was not the right pick for a child who has described fish as smelling like “stinky garbage,” but he did love using the included chopsticks. This is a fun, relatively affordable way to try to expand a kid's picky palate. —Adrienne So

This is coming with a big disclaimer: CrunchLabs, the company founded by former NASA engineer and YouTuber Mark Rober, says that its Hack Pack is for people over 14 years old. However, my 8-year-old put it together with minimal help in an afternoon, although it did take him much longer than the 60-minute estimated time for a teenager. ("Why are you so worried, Mama?" he admonished me when I showed skepticism. "The drawings show you exactly what to do.") Each box has a robot to assemble that illustrates a STEM principle, complete with a battery and an Arduino microcontroller. You can also use video instructions, but the drawings in the included manual were clear and big enough that we could put it together without the help of the internet.

Our box had the infrared turret to illustrate the principle of infrared communication. My 8-year-old was thrilled, hiding it and using the infrared remote to ambush his sister with a barrage of foam darts. You can also unlock a web-based coding console to hack the robot by locking out other users. It's expensive, but the price-per-payoff valuation is worth it—the STEM principle it illustrated was interesting, the build process was engaging without being frustrating, and the project itself was very fun. Do note that the subscription is annual, with different pay frequency options. —Adrienne So

CrunchLabs is great, but it is generally aimed at older kids. The Creative Kit subscription I tried is aimed at 6- to 10-year-olds, the youngest of the brand's offerings.

I'm shocked at how well-thought-out these kits are. The Creative Kit's we tried were the Ice Cream Stand and the Architect's Treehouse. The morning after the crates were assembled, both kiddos begged to get more. Even after my 8-year-old son had put together the tiny ice cream stand, he continued to play with it all afternoon. It comes with delightful little accessories, like moldable ice cream in different “flavors” (colors), little toppings, and tiny cones and cups. (I was offered a tiny scoop of “Super Strawberry Smash.”) Unlike most tie-dye or pinwheel kits, the Creative Kit encourages play long after the craft has been completed. My 10-year-old likes these better than the Kiwi Crates because “it's like building a tiny town!” —Adrienne So

My third-grader is a voracious reader. But it took him a few years and some hurdles to get to this point. In that time, the Lovevery Reading Skill Set was invaluable. These books and games are a complete homeschool reading curriculum aimed at pre-K through the second grade, but we used them as supplementary materials. Each set comes with three separate components: lovely, sturdy games; a few books; and parent course materials to explain both. You can buy an entire set together, or get a bi-monthly subscription, and there's three sets for different age groups.

The set was particularly crucial for my son during the second grade years. I particularly liked that the skill set addressed common errors and advanced vowel rules. It is really hard to remember what the letter “e” does in a word! My son found the board game to be very fun, like Chutes & Ladders but for word play. It was almost a disappointment when we realized, at the end of the summer, that he could actually read perfectly. —Adrienne So

As my kids have gotten older, shopping for their clothes has become more and more difficult. One child has sensory issues. He hates stiff or tight fabrics, clothes that are too fuzzy, certain colors, and all jackets. The other is a budding fashionista who is on a running team and dances and needs workout clothes, baggy jeans, or whatever else happens to not be in her closet at the moment. Whenever I find a sweatshirt they will wear, Target immediately discontinues it and my cycle of living hell continues.

Stitch Fix will cure what ails you, and you can start or stop your subscription as needed. You enter your child's age and size; color preferences and patterns to avoid; themes that they like; features to avoid (no crop tops); activities they like; and most importantly, budgets for every category. Stitch Fix sends you a box and you and your child keep what you like and send back the rest. You'll buy the individual items you like, and will pay a base $20 styling fee to get the box. I tested the box with my 10-year-old, and both of us liked the new picks. I was able to easily get an assortment of athletic clothing for her, as well as a cloud sweater she adores. This is an easy way to update wardrobes for kids who grow out of everything instantaneously and are really hard to shop for. —Adrienne So

Keke Pouch Subscription for $40: Kids are small factories of chaos. The constant stream of worksheets, drawings, and crafts is great for encouraging creativity and hand-eye coordination, but every surface in our house is perpetually cluttered. Rather than contributing to the mess, the Keke (Keep Every Kid Engaged) bag keeps every item organized. This frankly adorable, durable, handled bag has four activities in separate pouches that clip into binder rings, and extra storage pockets for masks or hand sanitizer. Dry-Brined Turkey With Tangy Honey Glaze

📅 2018-10-16  |  Read Source →

This dry-brine turkey recipe produces a Thanksgiving centerpiece that’s full of flavor and juicy as heck. The brine is just kosher salt and brown sugar, applied at least 12 hours and up to 2 days ahead. The salt helps the meat retain moisture, while an uncovered rest in the fridge dries the skin for better browning—no bucket required.

Roasting the bird on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet (instead of a high-sided roasting pan) lets hot air circulate around it, encouraging browning all over. We’ve also done away with the turkey baster in favor of a buttery honey-vinegar glaze you’ll brush over the bird while it cooks. Fragrant with rosemary, garlic, and orange zest, with Worcestershire sauce for savory depth, the repeated brushing builds an even, glossy lacquer.

Let the cooked turkey rest uncovered for 30 minutes to 1 hour before carving. Resist the urge to tent it with foil, which traps steam and softens that hard-earned crispy skin. Use the downtime to put the finishing touches on your Thanksgiving side dishes.

What kind of turkey should I buy for dry-brining?

Choose an unseasoned turkey. Kosher, self-basting, and enhanced turkeys have already been salted, so adding this dry brine can make them overly salty. Check the label for an added salt solution before buying. A frozen turkey is fine; thaw it in the refrigerator for at least 24 hours before starting the brine.

No. In this recipe, the brine stays on the bird; Step 2 calls for rinsing the baking sheet and rack to prevent the drippings from becoming too salty, not the turkey. Follow the measurement for your brand of kosher salt, and don’t feel compelled to use every last grain of the mixture—the recipe intentionally makes extra.

Yes, but taste them first: The dry brine and glaze make these drippings especially flavorful, and they may be quite salty. Skim off the excess fat to make your roux, then add the juices a little at a time, followed by low-sodium chicken broth (or homemade unseasoned turkey stock).

Our Make-Ahead Gravy doesn’t need drippings, but you can stir some in as it reheats. Taste before adding salt.

Can I use a larger or spatchcocked turkey?

Yes, but not with the same roasting schedule. This dry brine recipe is written for a whole 12–14-lb. bird; a larger turkey or one that’s been flattened will cook differently. For a spatchcocked bird, follow our Spatchcock Turkey With Anise and Orange rather than transferring this recipe’s roasting and glazing times.

See why each step works in our guide to cooking Thanksgiving turkey →

4 hours 45 minutes, plus at least 12 hours brining

Place ½ cup Diamond Crystal or ¼ cup plus 1½ tsp. Morton kosher salt and 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar in a small bowl and work together with your fingers until incorporated. Place one 12–14-lb. turkey, neck reserved for gravy, giblets discarded, on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet and pat dry with paper towels. (If you don’t have this setup, place your turkey on a V-shape rack set inside a large roasting pan.) Sprinkle salt mixture all over outside of the turkey as well as inside the cavity of the turkey, patting to adhere and nudging some into every crevice. You won’t need all of the dry brine, but it’s good to have extra since some of it will end up on the baking sheet as you season the turkey. Chill bird, uncovered, at least 12 hours and up to 2 days.

Remove turkey from wire rack and rinse baking sheet and rack if needed (turkey will most likely release some liquid onto pan). Line baking sheet with 3 layers of foil and set rack back inside. Place turkey, breast side up, on rack and tuck wings underneath. Let turkey sit at room temperature 2–3 hours.

Place an oven rack in middle of oven; preheat oven to 450°. Using your fingers, loosen turkey skin on breast. Work 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, under skin, spreading evenly over both breasts. Smear outside of turkey with another 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature.

Tie legs together with kitchen twine and pour 1 cup water into baking sheet.

Roast turkey, rotating pan halfway through, until skin is mostly golden brown all over, about 30 minutes.

Meanwhile, cook ¼ cup sherry vinegar or red wine vinegar, 2 Tbsp. honey, 4 tsp. Worcestershire sauce, 3 sprigs rosemary, 3 garlic cloves, crushed, two 2x1" strips orange zest, and remaining 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, in a small saucepan over medium heat until bubbling and slightly thickened, about 5 minutes. Reduce heat to lowest setting and keep glaze warm.

Reduce oven temperature to 300° and continue to roast turkey, brushing with glaze every 30 minutes and adding more water by ½-cupfuls as needed to maintain some liquid in baking sheet, until an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of breast near the neck registers 155° (don’t worry; the temperature will continue to climb while the bird rests), 65–85 minutes longer. Skin should be deep golden brown, shiny, and crisp. Transfer turkey to a cutting board and let rest at least 30 minutes and up to 1 hour before carving.

Editor’s note: The dry-brine turkey recipe was first printed in our November 2018 issue as ‘Dry-Rubbed Roast Turkey.’ Head this way for more of our best Thanksgiving recipes →

How to Fix Scratches on Wood Floors

📅 2018-10-12  |  Read Source →

By Andreana Lefton, Bob Vila, Updated by Tony Carrick

Updated Nov 6, 2026 5:08 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

While wood might be one of the longer-lasting flooring options for your home, hardwood floors certainly aren’t indestructible. Dog claws, dirt, hard-soled shoes, and heavy furniture can leave everything from minor scratches to deep gouges in your beautiful hardwood floors.

While you can counter these threats with furniture pads, a “no-shoes” rule, and regular trips to the dog groomer, chances are you’ll still have to deal with scratches from time to time. Fortunately, you don’t have to live with these unsightly imperfections. How do you fix scratched wood floors? Ahead, using a variety of wood floor repair options, including everything from superficial scratches to deep gouges.

A number of things can wreak havoc on wood floors:

Cleaning the floors regularly is key to keeping them in good condition. Use a lightweight cordless vacuum cleaner or a broom to remove any loose dirt.

“Your choice of cleaning tools also matters: Stiff-bristled brooms and vacuums with beater bars and wheels can scratch and ding your hardwood floors, even when used with the proper cleaning techniques,” Clavin says.

After vacuuming or sweeping, mop the floors using a mop for hardwood floors with a hardwood floor cleaner, such as Bona Hardwood Floor Cleaner Spray. Making sure that the cleaner you choose is rated for your type of hardwood floor will also help keep them free of grit.

While scratches in most types of hardwood flooring can often be repaired, in some cases the best option may be to rip up the floor and start anew. If the gouges are too deep to fix using the above methods, or if you’re dealing with widespread damage, then replacing the hardwood floor may be the only option. If they’re severely damaged structurally, or you have water damage, that’s often not fixable.

“Wood floors are more resilient than you think, and can often be brought back to life with a sanding and a stain,” Berres says. “However, if they’re severely damaged structurally, or you’ve got water damage, that’s often not fixable.”

If you choose to replace wood floors, floor installation costs an average of $3,067 for about 500 square feet.

While the methods above will help you repair scratches to wood floors, the steps below will help to prevent them from happening in the first place:

After months of scouring the market and putting products through their paces, we’ve named the best of the best in new tools. There’s something for everyone, from veteran pros to average Joes.

Dutch Apple Pie

📅 2018-08-22  |  Read Source →

Dutch apple pie is the upgrade the fall classic needs. With a nutty, streusel topping, this cozy dessert will have you coming back for slice after slice. It’s also easier to make than the traditional version because there’s no need to cook the apple filling ahead of time. In fact, some of us will be swapping out our traditional apple pie recipe for this one for Thanksgiving this year.

Lightly flour your work surface, then roll out the chilled dough into a 12-inch circle. Carefully move it to a 9-inch pie dish. Gently lift the edges and let the dough settle into the pan. Make sure not to stretch the dough. Press it firmly into the bottom and sides. Pop it in the fridge until it’s cold (about 30 minutes), or freeze it for 10 minutes if you’re in a hurry.

Grab a medium bowl and whisk together the granulated sugar, flour, cinnamon, and salt.

In a large bowl, whisk together the egg, sour cream, and vanilla. Add the dry ingredients and whisk until the mixture is nice and smooth.

Move a rack to the center of your oven and preheat it to 350°. In a medium bowl, whisk together the brown sugar, flour, granulated sugar, walnuts, oats, and cinnamon. Add the butter and use a fork or pastry cutter to work it in until crumbly.

Spread the apples evenly in your prepared pie crust. Pour the filling over the apples, then sprinkle the crumble topping all over.

Bake the pie until the crust and crumble are golden brown and you don’t see any more moisture. This should take about an hour. Once it’s done, let the pie cool on a wire rack until it reaches room temperature, then pop it in the fridge for an hour before serving.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Because this pie has a sour cream and egg-based filling, store leftovers covered in the refrigerator for up to 4 days.

The Top 9 Running Shorts That Prevent Chafing, Store All the Snacks, and Keep You Cool at Every Pace

📅 2018-08-16  |  Read Source →

Our editors tested several bottoms to determine the top shorts for runners.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

As a year-round shorts runner, I’m an expert in the good, the bad, and the miserable when it comes to running shorts. Comfort, potential for chafing, and storage are front-of-mind when I’m shopping for a pair. When runners look for their next pair, there are all kinds of questions to consider: What’s the right inseam to prevent friction? Are the pockets big enough to store my phone, sunscreen, and gels?

For all my hot summer runners and year-round leg barers, this guide is for you. We go over the best shorts that’ll have drawer life well beyond the first fall leaf, plenty of race-day storage for all the gels and gummies, and other features to look out for when shopping for bottoms.

Runner’s World has been testing products since 1966, vetting the best gear for runners so they can train hard, race their best, and enjoy all the other runs throughout their lifetime. Over the course of three weeks, we had our staff test several shorts to narrow down the best men’s and women’s bottoms during their workouts, from trail running to marathon training, from speed to recovery.

The RW team includes runners of varying levels (not all of us have run a 26.2!), ages, body types, heights, and running goals. We considered well-known shoe brands’ offerings, activewear-centric apparel, and lesser-known brands to give you a range of shorts styles and pricing. Our input lends a well-rounded collection of feedback so we can find shorts for every type of runner.

We awarded Tracksmith’s Session Shorts in our annual Gear of the Year, because they have all the essential features, including comfort, storage, and adjustability. The older version had a zippered side pocket that positioned the girth to the front, causing whatever you stored inside to hit your thigh as you ran.

The newest Session remedies that issue, with the girth set to the back. The waistband is also updated; it’s more flexible and has an internal drawstring, unlike the previous Session. The “Session 3.0” is great for training and racing; three rear drop-in gel pockets make fueling easy when you’re hours on the road, and the silky material provides plenty of airflow.

Shop Men’s at tracksmith.com | Shop Women’s at tracksmith.com

The RC Essential 3” is a short made for racing—at a Best Value price. The amount of storage alone makes the RC Essential a winner; individual compartments along the waistband make separating your gels easier (no more caf or no-caf surprises midrun). The four drop-in compartments are complete with a zippered rear pocket for storing discards (though the pocket is large enough to store a phone, doing so results in jostling).

I tested the short and loved the short inseam and split-leg design. The lightweight, quick-drying fabric alone makes these shorts not a distraction, but I can’t help thinking the lack of fabric on my thighs makes me stride better—and faster.

Shop Men’s at newbalance.com | Shop Women’s at newbalance.com

Runners flock to the cheap Amazon brand Baleaf because its threads are comfy and check all the boxes when it comes to affordable, functional activewear. Take the men’s Running Athletic Shorts: these bottoms are adjustable, breathable, have inclusive sizing, lots of storage, and just feel dang comfy.

“The shorts performed very well,” said a tester. “My first time wearing these was at a local 5K in August, and the shorts did not hold me back in any way. The drawstring made the shorts nice and secure. I carried only my keys in the zipper pocket on the back, and there were no issues there either. I continued running in them on various types of runs following the race and it was more of the same. Great performance and very comfortable.”

Sweat-wicking mesh briefs provide support, while an internal drawcord allows you to adjust the waistband to ensure a secure, nonslip fit. You may need to tighten that drawcord too, since these shorts run large. They also run quite long; if you usually wear a 30-inch inseam or shorter in pants, these are not the shorts for you.

There are two side pockets and one lower back zipper pocket; all are sizable for a phone, in case you’re a thigh phone pocket runner or the more advanced lower back pocket runner.

These Old Navy shorts are at a jaw-dropping price—and our testers were impressed with how they performed on their runs. Even though the inseam is only 3 inches, Assistant Commerce Photo Editor Saba Ahmed reported no thigh chafing on her runs. “These are definitely the shortest inseam running shorts I own and I surprisingly really enjoyed running in these, especially on an almost 80-degree sunny day!” she said.

The elastic waistband has a drawstring for adjustability. The poly-spandex material felt especially breathable, even on very hot days. They also dry extremely quickly. These are Senior Gear Editor Cat Bowen’s shorts of choice to wear on rainy summer days. The only downside with the shorts was the one side pocket. “I was pretty confused about why there was only one pocket on one side,” said Ahmed. “I appreciated that it had a zip, but I found myself trying to find a pocket on the left side as well and then remembering there was none.”

The 2-in-1 short is a divisive topic (like wearing underwear under your shorts), but tester and RW Editor Matt Rudisill said these shorts by Janji felt like running in half tights.

“The tights portion fit very well,” he said. “Not constricting and not loose. They found that perfect middle ground where they’re snug but they didn’t cause any issues (no chafing, sliding around, etc.). The pocket situation was fantastic.”

The Multi Short could very well be our Best for Storage short, as well. The waistband has five compartments for all the gels, gummies, and Sour Patch Kids you crave on your runs. The legs have snug side pockets to store your phone.

Rudisill was delighted to find one of the waistband pockets had an attached S-Biner MicroLock carabiner inside to stash your keys.

For Your Consideration: Ladies, if you want a 2-in-1 yourselves, we also tested the Ciele DLYShort 4” Long Brief 2, which changed Editor Ashley Tysiac’s mind about 2-in-1 shorts.

“Not only were they comfortable for any workout duration or speed, but I never had to worry about discomfort or chafing,” she said. “In a way, they gave me peace of mind. I could throw them on, and knew that they would feel great and perform well, no matter the workout, distance, or pace.”

Have you fallen victim to the dreaded crotch sweat mark postrun? Enter the Bandit Vanisher collection, which promises threads that don’t show sweat.

Both the men’s and women’s shorts have seven waterproof pockets so your phone won’t become sweat-logged, because even though these shorts don’t show marks, they still retain moisture. The cuffs also have silicone grippers so the legs won’t slide up.

“The apparel does exactly as it promises: it makes sweat vanish,” Features Senior Editor Pavlína Černá wrote in her review. “I ran 9 miles in 80-degree heat one weekend and 10 miles the weekend after, and while my skin was glistening, there was not a single sweat mark on my outfit.”

Shop Men’s at bandit.com | Shop Women’s at bandit.com

The Hotty Hot High-Rise is a classic flow short that we love for all kinds of workouts—especially fast ones. The cut is so flattering with mesh panels on the sides and reflective detail in case you’re an early riser or dusk dancer.

We wish there was more room for storage, but the liner stash pocket and zippered pocket on the side provide just enough room to stash keys and a card.

The flat, high waistband has an interior infinity cord for adjusting.

For men, we like the Pace Breaker Lined Short, which comes in five-, seven-, and nine-inch inseams. The short is updated for a comfier fit around the waist and low-bounce hand pockets.

The airy Smartwool Active Lined achieves a miracle you rarely see in short-seamed, flowy shorts: it doesn’t cause chafing. Senior Gear Editor Cat Bowen praised the shorts for their ample storage room, breathable liner, and ability to stay put as she runs.

Bowen reported no bunching or riding up of the leg openings. An added bonus: the shorts launder well without falling apart. And even though you should wash your shorts after a sweaty session (read: bacteria), the liner is efficient at wicking moisture and not gathering odor—so only you know you’re late on laundry day.

Shop Men’s at smartwool.com | Shop Women’s at smartwool.com

While testing the Twilight Short Tights, I honestly couldn’t stop brushing the palms of my hands against my thighs. The textured fabric, a nylon/elastane blend, is ultrasoft.

I wore the shorts during my marathon training and appreciated the storage they provided for fuel and my phone.

Editorial Assistant Michael McDonough was surprised at the Twilight’s generous storage space despite having only three pockets when he tested the men’s version, the Twilight Half Tights.

“Though it may not seem like it at first glance, the Tracksmith Half Tights have great storage capacity,” he said. “Their three pockets (two drop-in side pockets and a rear zippered pocket) look skinny—very skinny. The side pockets are just over 2.5 inches wide, and the rear pocket runs just over 3.5 inches at its max depth. But the secret to the pockets is their stretch. My phone is more than three inches wide, but it was still able to fit in the side pocket.”

Shop Men’s at tracksmith.com | Shop Women’s at tracksmith.com

It’s about comfort and intention. Some runners prefer a bike short (compression or half-tight, as these shorts are also called) to ensure the legs don’t ride up. Many brands add grip tape on the inside of the cuffs to make the legs stay secure.

On the other hand, a flowy short (or loose or relaxed fit) provides more airflow and can come in a split-leg style, which some runners prefer for racing to enhance mobility.

Inseams can be as little as 1.25 inches or as long as 10 inches for maximum coverage. Some runners prefer a longer inseam to prevent the dreaded thigh chafing, while others want as little leg coverage as possible for racing.

To run with underwear or commando is a question RW staff heatedly discusses every so often. The right liner wicks away moisture and doesn’t cause a wedgie or camel toe midrun. If you prefer to run with underwear, who’s to know? But there are some shorts out there sans liner.

Another hot topic besides running with or without underwear: Storing your phone in the thigh or back waistband pocket? If you run with a phone, location is one thing to consider as well as security. After all, no one wants to feel their phone constantly hitting their leg as they run.

Brands are also finally understanding that both men and women need pockets. Some shorts come with 360 waistband storage; others have deep side pockets. Lastly, if you carry small valuables like keys or an ID, you’ll want to consider zippered or envelope pockets over drop-ins.

We included some options for runners on a budget, but we understand our top picks are up there in cost. These shorts are made of durable, sweat-wicking materials that should last you for races and years to come. But we understand not every runner can afford such an investment.

Some ways to make your shorts last longer are washing without fabric softener (you should do this with all workout clothes so it doesn’t degrade sweat-wicking capabilities) and air drying.

How do I prevent my inner thighs from chafing?

If your runs tend to end with chafed thighs, my first recommendation is to try some shorts with a longer inseam. I find that a 7-inch, or half-tight length, usually provides enough coverage to prevent thigh rubbing. Bike shorts with interior strips on the cuffs also help by keeping the legs from riding up.

If you don't like a long inseam, you can also apply Vaseline or BodyGlide to trouble spots, which will minimize friction where you usually chafe.

Is running in shorts in the winter bad for your knees?

As a runner who resists wearing leggings for as long as I can before hypothermia sets in, I spoke with Shane M. Davis, MD, when I noticed how my knees ached on winter runs. Davis, who is a physiatrist of non-operative medicine at Tufts University School of Medicine in Boston, told me that the cold affects the elasticity of your tissues. You may also experience arthritic joint pain as the weather changes due to decreased blood flow.

If, like me, you're adamant about training in shorts when there’s snow on the ground, one way to prevent joint pain is doing dynamic stretches, like high knees and toe-and-heel walking, before your run.

Should I go commando or wear underwear when wearing running shorts?

This is a hot topic at RW HQ: Some shorts have built-in support, but you should definitely try them on before making a commitment. If you're already getting a wedgie before you start to run, they’re going to be a nuisance during a workout.

Some brands make no-liner shorts, so you can wear underwear instead of an attached liner. Alternatively, 2-in-1 shorts offer more coverage like a bike short with a flowy outer layer for added protection.

Amanda Furrer, Editor, Running Reviews, studied journalism at NYU and writing at Emerson College. She has reviewed gear and covered other topics in the running space for almost 10 years. Since 2013, she has consecutively run the Boston Marathon. She also has a master’s degree in gastronomy from Boston University and was formerly a professional baker for two years before hanging up her apron.

The 8 Best Cordless Stick Vacuums Are Convenient, Versatile, and Work Well on Carpets Too

📅 2018-08-03  |  Read Source →

These lightweight, easy-to-store models get the job done with multiple attachments and powerful motors.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

A cordless stick vacuum has the potential to be one of the most convenient cleaning tools in your home. These lightweight machines make it easy to clean up pet hair from area rugs and hidden dust along baseboards, without having to haul out an upright vacuum or wrestling with a power cord. Most models also convert into handheld vacuums, giving you more flexibility to clean furniture, stairs, and other hard-to-reach spots.

Suction power, battery life, maneuverability, and dustbin capacities vary between models, so the right cordless stick vacuum ultimately depends on your home’s flooring, how much debris you’re dealing with, and the features that will actually make your cleaning routine easier.

We’ve rounded up the best cordless stick vacuums worth considering based on our hands-on experience, with options for everything from everyday maintenance to deeper carpet cleaning.

🔎 Looking for more ways to keep your floors and furniture clean? Check out our stories on the best robot vacuums, the best vacuums for pet hair, the best vacuums for hardwood floors, and the best handheld vacuums.

I’ve spent considerable time researching, testing, and vetting all types of vacuums, so I’ve developed a pretty good understanding of what separates a genuinely useful appliance from one that is just going to take up space in your storage closet. I’ve evaluated a wide range of models, including full-size uprights, canister vacuums, robot, and handheld models vacuums, paying close attention to performance, ease of use, and features that can really make a difference in a your regular cleaning routine.

When testing cordless stick vacuums, we assessed how well each model could handle common household debris, its ability to transition between carpet and hardwood floors, and how easy it was to move around furniture and into tighter spaces. We also looked at the practical considerations like battery life, suction power, dustbin capacity, weight, filtration, and included attachments.

Beyond just how the vacuum cleans, we also considered the overall experience for an owner. Removable batteries, self-cleaning docking stations, washable filters, and easy-to-empty dustbins can also make daily vacuuming less of a drag. The below recommendations are both powerful and dependable for a wide range of cleaning needs and surface types.

The Dyson V15 Detect is the most expensive stick vacuum in our lineup, but if you can stomach the price tag, you’ll get a cordless stick vacuum that (quite literally) leaves the competition in the dust across the board. It delivers top-notch performance on floors and carpets to tackle everything from fine dust and pet dander to larger debris.

Its motor spins up to 125,000 rpm and it comes with two cleaning heads, including an upgraded version of the brand’s “fluffy” head that features a green laser headlight to detect fine dust. During testing, we found that being able to switch from cordless to handheld made it easier to transition from tackling small messes to deeper cleaning sessions.

The auto mode is a nice feature for conserving battery life, as this model only runs for about an hour off one charge. Another highlight is the motorized pet tool.

Full Dyson V15 Detect Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

The PowerClean FurFinder is a more affordable alternative to the PowerClean FurGuard we recommend, but that doesn’t mean you have to give up useful features.

This cordless stick vacuum is intended for use on a variety of floor types, and the flat-lay construction makes cleaning under furniture much more accessible. The vacuum also stands up on its own which leads to fewer interruptions during a cleaning session.

We love that both the main vacuum head and pet hair attachment come with headlights because it makes it a lot easier to see hidden dirt and debris. The brush roll is a tangle-free design, and the FurFinder is equipped with a HEPA filter for trapping small particles and allergens.

The motor on this vacuum is less powerful than the PowerClean, but it still provides plenty of suction for daily household cleaning.

Full Bissell PowerClean FurFinder Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

Since we’re big fans of the Shark AZ2002 Vertex upright vacuum for deep and thorough cleans, it’s no surprise that we like the cordless model in this lineup, too. The brush roll is excellent at pulling hair and large debris from carpets, as well as polishing up your hardwood floors. The cordless stick vacuum features an advertised runtime of 60 minutes.

It also offers a HEPA filtration system and converts to a hand vac, and it also bends at the center of the wand to get around and under furniture. The Vertex comes with plenty of accessories for cleaning a variety of spaces. However, this may not be the best choice if you regularly clean stairs, as the cordless vacuum weighs roughly nine pounds—significantly more than other vacuums we cover.

I have two cats and live with my partner, which means there is plenty of mess to go around, from pet hair and dust to tracked-in dirt. I appreciate all the thoughtful features of the Tineco Station 5 because they make daily maintenance much easier.

Automatic suction adjustment has proved useful on my laminate and tile floors. I can hear when the motor gets a boost of power in a particularly dirty area, and it helps on carpets and area rugs where crumbs and debris tends to get trapped between the fibers. Add to that a green laser light for dust detection and you’ll hardly ever miss a spot.

I particularly like the base station. It has a 2.5-liter capacity, and after two months of using the Station 5, I still haven’t had to empty it. When I dock the vacuum onto the base, it automatically empties the onboard dustbin, cleans the floorhead brush roll, and charges the vacuum. Combined, these intuitive features make for a cleaning routine that is virtually hands-free.

Full Tineco Pure ONE Station 5 Pro Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

Even though the Dyson V11 Origin came out several years ago, that doesn’t mean this model has stopped being useful.

The motorbar cleaner head works exceptionally well on carpet, successfully agitating fibers to pull up embedded dirt, dander, and hair. Suction is equally impressive, and there are three power modes depending on the level of cleaning you need. I ran the vacuum on medium most of the time, but I used boost occasionally whenever an area needed the extra attention.

This model doesn’t come with a soft roller brush vacuum head, and while it does work on all floor types, it can be loud on hardwood and laminate. But if your home is mostly carpet, this is a cordless stick vacuum I would reach for.

Full Dyson V11 Origin Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

Our tester has a busy household six pets, so there’s no question that she’s regularly cleaning up after her animals. Pulling out the upright vacuum daily isn’t convenient, so she turned to the Levoit LVAC-300 to fill the gap.

This cordless stick vacuum has three different cleaning modes—eco, medium, and max—and she was able to cycle through each right from the handle depending on how much suction power she needed. She found that the vacuum was able to pick up litter and sand on different surfaces and between thresholds with ease.

The attachments are useful for cleaning curtains, cushions, and staircases, but she was disappointed that there’s nowhere to store these tools on the vacuum itself. The LVAC-300 runs for up to 60 minutes depending on what mode you’re using, and the HEPA filter is a great feature if you have animals.

Full Levoit LVAC-300 Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

I used the Bissell PowerClean FurGuard across my apartment to clean laminate floors, area rugs, carpeting, and upholstery, and I fell in love with several features.

The vacuum lays completely flat, making it really easy to get underneath furniture, and the swivel steering helps guide the FurGuard around tight spaces. Being a pet owner, I appreciate that the brush roll is tangle-free, so I don’t have to cut hair loose as often as I would with a standard, bristled brush roll.

Other highlights were lights on the main vacuum head and pet hair attachment. I was able to see collected dirt and debris that I would normally miss on hardwood flooring, carpet, and furniture. I also like that this model is designed to be self-standing.

It’s hard to come by a cordless stick vacuum that you don’t have to re-mount or set on the ground during your cleaning session. With this model, I was able to stop and start with ease.

Full Bissell PowerClean FurGuard Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

We recently got our hands on one of the latest cordless stick vacuums from Shark, and we were impressed by the PowerDetect’s construction. The pole has a hinge in the center and when you release it, the vacuum bends to extend under furniture and hard-to-reach places. Our tester didn’t have to kneel down or adjust angles while he was cleaning thanks to the intuitive design.

Beyond that, the PowerDetect vacuum uses sensors on the bottom of the floor head to discern between surface types, and adjusts suction power when needed. During testing, the motor could be heard transitioning speeds when used on hardwood and shag carpet.

This vacuum also comes with a docking station, which automates the process of emptying out the dustbin. The vacuum itself can’t hold that much debris onboard, so it’s nice having a one-stop-shop for clearing out the bagless canister during a cleaning session. It’s a little heavy for a cordless stick vacuum, but it still gets the job done.

Full Shark PowerDetect Cordless Stick Vacuum Review

Not every cordless stick vacuum we test ends up in this roundup. However, these other models still perform well and are reliable recommendations:

Levoit Aero Cordless Vacuum Cleaner: The Tineco we’ve included above has more raw suction power, reaching up to 27,000Pa versus the 14,000Pa of the Levoit. However, one of the highlights of the Aero is the 3.5-liter-capacity dust bag in the docking station. Depending on your cleaning frequency, you could potentially go months without having to empty and replace it, and this design is better for keeping dust and dander from being expelled back into the air.

Dyson Gen5Detect Cordless Stick Vacuum: This cordless stick vacuum certainly commands a premium price, and you get a lot of the same features as the Dyson V15 Detect we recommend above, such as an LCD display screen, the Fluffy Optic cleaner head, and pet hair attachments. If you’re an allergy sufferer, you may prefer the Gen5Detect because its filtration system captures particles down to 0.1 microns, while the V15 filters down to just 0.3 microns. Plus, runtime is slightly longer on the Gen5Detect.

The main advantages of owning a cordless stick vacuum are easy portability and storage. But what you trade for the convenience is battery life and runtime. Most models clean from 30 to 60 minutes, but that’s also dependent on what setting you’re using. If you’re running the vacuum on its highest speed the entire time, it might die faster. Be wary of manufacturers’ advertised total runtime because it may represent performance on the lowest setting or using two fully charged batteries, for instance. Battery charging should also be factored in, so plan your cleaning session accordingly so you don’t lose power in the middle of a floor swipe.

Like we mentioned earlier, the dustbins on cordless stick vacuums are significantly smaller than those of an upright or canister unit. You should consider your cleaning habits and the spaces you usually vacuum to find a model that aligns with your needs. If you live in an apartment or are using the cordless vac to supplement your other cleaning supplies, then a dustbin with at least half a liter capacity should do. Some of the models we recommend hold up to one liter of debris, which helps limit the number of trips to the trash can.

Although not a necessity, having a good amount of attachments with your vacuum is especially helpful for cleaning hard-to-reach places or targeting specific issues. Crevice tools pick up dirt and dander from corners and couch cushions, while bristled brushes and motorized pet tools agitate and lift hair and fur that’s been embedded into upholstery.

A good filtration system helps combat allergens such as dust and pet dander that are being kicked up by a vacuum. The best option is a HEPA filter, or “high-efficiency particulate air” filter, which traps 99.97 percent of particles that are 0.3 microns or larger in size. A fully sealed or multi-step filtration system also has the potential to trap a wide variety of airborne allergens without releasing them back into your home.

Cordless vacuums are very effective at picking up pet hair, especially models designed with high suction power and specialized brush rolls. Some of the models we recommend include motorized attachments that lift embedded hair from upholstery and carpets. Anti-tangle vacuum heads are also a key feature that helps prevent hair from wrapping around the brush roll. While not all models perform equally, a high-quality cordless stick vacuum is able to handle pet hair just as well as traditional versions.

The lifespan of these vacuums typically lasts from three to five years, depending on usage, build quality, and maintenance. Battery performance plays a major role, since lithium-ion batteries start to lose capacity over time.

Avoiding overcharging and storing the vacuum properly could help extend the battery life significantly. Running regular maintenance like cleaning the vacuum filters and emptying the dustbin completely preserves suction and functionality. Higher-end models often last longer due to better materials and replaceable parts, but either price point has potential to last for years with proper care.

Modern designs are increasingly more effective at cleaning many surface types, including rugs and carpets. Advances in battery tech and general efficiency have allowed for strong suction performance that is comparable to corded models. Adjustable power modes allow for deeper cleaning on thicker carpets, and multi-roller and motorized brush heads agitate fibers to loosen dirt and debris.

Performance may vary depending on the type of flooring. Some vacuums are designed to work on low-to-medium pile carpets, while others are able to handle high-pile. It’s best to consult the listed features and specs of the model you’re considering to ensure it aligns with the flooring types in your home.

Amber is a Reviews Editor with bylines on Popular Mechanics, Runner’s World, Bicycling, and Best Products sites. Specializing in kitchen gadgets, small appliances, lifestyle, and consumer tech, she brings hands-on testing and a detail-oriented approach to every review. In her free time you can likely find her trading trinkets at an EDM festival, searching for hidden gems at thrift stores, or cuddling with her two cats.
